lang/common.py 128 lines

Helpers shared by the tree-sitter based adapters for C#, Go, Kotlin and TypeScript/JavaScript: tree walking, hashing, string and URL handling, and constructors for the output records.

How it works

  • Tree access. parser(lang) builds a tree-sitter Parser for a grammar; text_of decodes a node's text (empty string for None); walk yields a node and all its descendants in source order using an explicit stack.
  • Change detection. token_hash is the SHA-1 (16 hex digits) of the space-joined leaf tokens of a node, excluding comment nodes. Whitespace and comments therefore never change a function's hash.
  • String literals. string_value returns the content of a plain string literal for the node types the four grammars use, and None if the literal contains an interpolation child or the node is not a string. It accepts double-quoted (optionally @-prefixed), single-quoted and back-quoted forms.
  • URLs. url_parts classifies a literal: an absolute URL gives (path, "host", hostname, None); a string starting with / gives (path, None, None, "base URL not present in call"); anything else gives (None, None, None, "path does not start with a literal segment"). template_path does the same for a list of parts, each either ("s", text) or ("v", base_ref_or_None): a leading variable is the base (and must be resolvable, else "base expression not resolvable"), a leading absolute URL supplies the host, later variables become {}, the query string is cut, and the path must start with /. Example: [("v", ("config_key", "ORDERS_URL")), ("s", "/orders/"), ("v", None)] gives ("/orders/{}", "config_key", "ORDERS_URL", None).
  • Record constructors. add_endpoint, add_callsite, add_function, add_test and add_schema append the corresponding data class to the extract with the id formats used everywhere: endpoint service:METHOD norm_path, call site repo:path:line, function and test repo:path::qualname, schema service:schema:Name. add_function stores the same token hash in hash and body_hash. add_schema does nothing when there are no fields. add_test keeps at most 60 literals.

Key functions and classes

NameLineWhat it does
parser17Creates a tree-sitter parser for a grammar.
text_of / walk21 / 25Node text; depth-first traversal.
token_hash33Comment- and whitespace-insensitive hash of a node.
string_value39Value of a non-interpolated string literal.
new_extract53Empty RepoExtract for one file.
url_parts57Literal URL or path to (path, base_kind, base_value, reason).
template_path67Parts list to the same four-tuple.
add_endpoint / add_callsite / add_function / add_test / add_schema91 / 101 / 107 / 112 / 117Append one record with the standard id format.
is_test_path122Generic test-path test (/test/, _test.go, .spec.ts, Tests.cs, Test.kt, ...).
VALIDATE_RULES127Map from go-playground validator rule names to constraint keys.

Edge cases and failure handling

  • The path normalisation and gateway-prefix stripping are delegated to normalize_path in models.py; a call site with no path stores norm_path = None together with the reason.
  • is_test_path and VALIDATE_RULES are not used by the four adapters in this directory: each adapter has its own test-file rule, and golang.py uses an inline copy of the rule map.
  • string_value does not unescape backslash sequences; the raw text between the quotes is returned.

lang/csharp.py 323 lines

The C#/.NET adapter: ASP.NET Core controller routes and minimal APIs, DTOs with DataAnnotations, HttpClient call sites, xUnit/NUnit/MSTest tests, and Entity Framework entities and data access.

How it works

Parser. tree-sitter with the tree_sitter_c_sharp grammar; a few facts (named clients, ToTable, Entity Framework access) are found with regular expressions over node text. Attributes on a declaration are read by _attrs (line 40) into a dict of name (namespace and Attribute suffix removed) to argument nodes.

  1. Named clients. A pre-pass records variable = x.CreateClient("orders") for assignments anywhere and for declarations outside methods; declarations inside a method are added only for that method. A call on such a variable gets base ("config_key", "orders"); a leading underscore on either side is ignored when matching (_client matches client).
  2. DTO schemas. For every class and record (including nested ones), record header parameters and properties without [JsonIgnore] become fields. The wire name is [JsonPropertyName("x")] / [JsonProperty("x")], else the property name with a lower-case first letter (_wire). Classes whose name ends in Controller, Tests or Test are not schemas.
  3. Entity Framework. A class whose base list names DbContext, IdentityDbContext or ApiAuthorizationDbContext contributes one out.repositories record of kind ef_dbset per DbSet<T> property, with the table from Entity<T>().ToTable("x") if present anywhere in the file. Any other class (not ending in Controller, Tests, Test, Service, Repository, Context) that has scalar properties (types matching EF_SCALAR, line 32) is recorded as a candidate Entity of kind ef: column name from [Column("x")] or the property name, key if [Key] or named Id / <Class>Id, nullable if the type ends in ? or is a string without [Required], [NotMapped] skipped. The table is [Table("x")] or left unresolved for resolve_data to settle.
  4. Methods. Each method directly in a class body becomes a Function (or a test). Its body is scanned for invocations.
  5. Controller endpoints and minimal APIs are emitted as described below.
DataAnnotationField result (_dto_field, line 78)
[Required], or type int, long, decimal, double, bool, Guid, DateTimerequired: True; a nullable type (?) is required only with [Required] and gets nullable: True
[StringLength(n, MinimumLength = m)], [MaxLength(n)], [MinLength(n)]max_length, min_length
[Range(lo, hi)]minimum, maximum
[RegularExpression("...")], [EmailAddress]pattern, format: "email"
type List<, IList<, IEnumerable<, ICollection<, IReadOnlyList< or an arraymulti: True

Controller routes. The class prefix is [Route("...")] with [controller] replaced by the class name minus Controller, lower-cased. A method with [HttpGet], [HttpPost], [HttpPut], [HttpDelete], [HttpPatch] or [HttpHead] is an endpoint; the sub-path is the attribute's string argument, else the method's own [Route]; a sub-path starting with / ignores the prefix.

[ApiController, Route("api/[controller]")]
public class OrdersController : ControllerBase {
    [HttpGet("{id}")]
    public async Task<ActionResult<OrderDto>> Get(int id) { ... }
}

gives Endpoint(method="GET", path="/api/orders/{id}", handler="OrdersController.Get", response_schema="OrderDto", response_shape="object", params=[{"name": "id", "in": "path", "type": "int", "required": True}]). _inner (line 109) unwraps Task, ValueTask, ActionResult, IActionResult, Results, Ok, then a collection type or array (shape list). Request schemas are the types of [FromBody] parameters, or, for POST/PUT/PATCH with none, the type of the last parameter if it starts with an upper-case letter. Every remaining parameter without [FromServices] is listed as a path parameter (if {name occurs in the route) or a query parameter, required unless its type ends in ?. Auth markers are [Authorize] and [AllowAnonymous] on class or method.

Minimal APIs. Any call whose last name is MapGet, MapPost, MapPut, MapDelete or MapPatch with a literal route becomes an endpoint. The handler name is the source text of the second argument (or "lambda"), and RequireAuthorization() is recorded if .RequireAuthorization( appears in the text of the node two levels above the call.

Outbound calls. A call whose method name is in CLIENT (line 27: GetAsync, GetStringAsync, GetFromJsonAsync, PostAsync, PostAsJsonAsync, PutAsync, DeleteAsync, PatchAsync, ...) is a call site regardless of the receiver's type. _interp (line 125) resolves the first argument: a plain literal through url_parts; an interpolated string or a + concatenation that starts with a literal, with later expressions as {} (a leading literal without / gets one prepended, since HttpClient resolves it against BaseAddress). Example: _orders.GetAsync($"/api/orders/{id}"), where _orders = factory.CreateClient("orders"), gives CallSite(method="GET", norm_path="/api/orders/{}", base_kind="config_key", base_value="orders").

Tests. A class whose text contains [Fact, [Theory, [Test or [TestMethod is a test class. Methods in it with a test attribute become TestCase records, and client calls in them become http_calls instead of call sites.

Entity Framework access. In each non-test method the body text is matched for x.Orders.Where( style chains and Set<Order>().Find; method names in EF_WRITES give write, in EF_READS give read. The result is a data_refs record keyed by DbSet name or model name, resolved in resolve_data.

Edge cases and failure handling

  • A file is reported as failed ("C# source does not parse") only if the tree has errors and contains no class or record at all; a file with local syntax errors is still analysed.
  • The docstring lists SendAsync among recognised client methods, but CLIENT (lines 27-29) does not contain it, so SendAsync calls produce no call site.
  • A URL that begins with an expression ($"{baseUrl}/orders") is recorded with the reason "URL starts with an expression"; the base variable is not looked up. The named_clients parameter of _interp is not used.
  • Only methods directly in a class body are functions. Constructors and top-level statements (for example the lambdas passed to MapGet) are not, so a minimal-API endpoint's handler_id (built from the handler's source text, line 322) does not refer to any extracted Function.
  • Framework parameters such as CancellationToken are listed as query parameters, because only [FromServices] and body types are excluded.
  • Test cases carry no imports, statuses or literals.

lang/golang.py 236 lines

The Go adapter: routes for net/http, chi, gin, echo and gorilla/mux, struct schemas from tags, net/http client call sites, and Test* functions.

How it works

Parser. tree-sitter with the tree_sitter_go grammar, with regular expressions for struct tags, fmt.Sprintf and environment lookups.

  1. Base variables. For every variable or constant declaration and assignment, if the value contains os.Getenv("X") or os.LookupEnv("X") the first declared name maps to ("config_key", "X"); if the value is exactly an http(s):// string literal it maps to ("literal", url).
  2. Schemas. Every named struct type becomes a schema. The field name is the first element of the json:"..." tag, or the Go field name when there is no tag; json:"-" fields and embedded (unnamed) fields are skipped. A type starting with [] sets multi; a pointer type sets nullable. Rules come from validate:"..." and binding:"..." tags (_field_rules, line 31): required; email; oneof=a b gives allowed; max/min give max_length/min_length; gt, gte, lt, lte give exclusive or inclusive bounds. Example: Name string `json:"name" validate:"required,max=50"` gives {"name": {"type": "string", "required": True, "constraints": {"max_length": 50}}}.
  3. Functions. Each top-level function or method becomes a Function named Receiver.Name or Name; in a _test.go file a function starting with Test becomes a TestCase instead. The body's call expressions are classified by the table below.
  4. Request and response schemas. While scanning a body, local variable types are tracked (var x T, x := T{, x := &T{, x := []T{). Decode(&v), ShouldBindJSON(&v), BindJSON, ShouldBind, Bind record v's type as the function's request type; json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(v) and c.JSON(status, v) / IndentedJSON record the response type. After all functions are processed, each endpoint whose handler_id equals a function id receives these as request_schemas, response_schema and response_shape (list for a slice).
CallFact
X.HandleFunc("GET /orders/{id}", h), X.Handle(pattern, h)endpoint; a Go 1.22 "VERB /path" pattern supplies the verb; a chained gorilla .Methods("GET", "POST") supplies one endpoint per verb; default GET
r.Get("/x", h) (chi: Get, Post, Put, Delete, Patch, Head) or r.GET("/x", h) (gin, echo: upper-case verb), with at least two arguments and a literal pathendpoint; the handler is the last argument; skipped when the receiver root is http, client, c or ctx

URL resolution (_url, line 190). A literal goes through url_parts. fmt.Sprintf("%s/orders/%d", base, id) is split on %s, %d, %v, %q: a placeholder in first position is the base, resolved by _base (os.Getenv("X") or a variable from step 1; a literal URL variable gives its host); later placeholders become {}. A + concatenation is treated the same way. Example: with base := os.Getenv("ORDERS_URL"), http.Get(base + "/orders/" + id) gives CallSite(method="GET", norm_path="/orders/{}", base_kind="config_key", base_value="ORDERS_URL").

Edge cases and failure handling

  • A file is reported as failed ("Go source does not parse") only when the tree has errors and no top-level function or method exists.
  • Handler lookup uses the handler argument's source text against a map keyed by bare function name (lines 109 and 144). A method-value handler such as s.listOrders is not found, so the endpoint's handler_id becomes repo:path::s.listOrders, which does not equal the function id repo:path::Server.listOrders; the request and response schemas found in that handler are then not attached.
  • The route rule at lines 145-146 does not check what the receiver is beyond excluding four names. A client call such as apiClient.Post("/orders", "application/json", body) satisfies it (method in CHI, literal first argument, two or more arguments) and is recorded as a POST /orders endpoint with handler body, in addition to the call site produced by line 163.

lang/kotlin.py 197 lines

The Kotlin adapter for Spring: controller endpoints, DTOs from constructor properties with Bean Validation, RestTemplate and WebClient call sites, and @Test functions.

How it works

Parser. tree-sitter (tree_sitter_kotlin) locates classes, objects, functions, parameters and call expressions; annotations and their arguments are then read from node text with the regex ANN (line 23), which accepts an optional use-site target (@field:, @param:, @get:) and one level of nested parentheses. _anns returns a dict of annotation name to raw argument text.

  1. Config-bound properties. A regex over the whole file maps each property declared as @Value("\${key}") private lateinit var name (modifiers optional, default after : dropped) to ("config_key", key).
  2. Classes and objects. For each one, class annotations are read from its modifiers node and, for top-level classes, also from the file text between the previous top-level class and this one.
  3. DTO schemas. Each constructor parameter declared with val or var and not private is a field. _field (line 44): required if it has @NotNull, @NotBlank or @NotEmpty, or if its type is not nullable (no trailing ?); @Size(min, max), @Min/@Max, @DecimalMin/@DecimalMax, @Pattern(regexp), @Email, @Positive map to the same constraint keys as in Java; @NotBlank/@NotEmpty imply min_length: 1. Classes annotated RestController, Controller, Service, Component or Repository are not schemas. Example: data class NewOwner(@field:NotBlank @field:Size(max = 30) val name: String, val phone: String?) gives {"name": {"type": "String", "required": True, "constraints": {"max_length": 30, "min_length": 1}}, "phone": {"type": "String?", "required": False, "nullable": True}}.
  4. Functions. Each function directly in a class body becomes a Function named Class.function, or a TestCase if the file is a test file (/src/test/, *Test.kt, *Tests.kt) and the function has @Test.
  5. Endpoints. A function with @GetMapping, @PostMapping, @PutMapping, @DeleteMapping, @PatchMapping, or @RequestMapping (verbs from RequestMethod.X, default GET) is an endpoint. The path is the class @RequestMapping first string joined with the function annotation's first string. Request schemas are the types of @RequestBody parameters; @PathVariable and @RequestParam parameters are listed, required unless the type is nullable. The return type is read by regex from the function text; one wrapper (ResponseEntity, Mono, List, Set, Collection, Flux) is removed; shape is list for collection types. Auth markers are @PreAuthorize, @Secured, @RolesAllowed on class or function.
  6. Outbound calls (non-test files only). A call named getForObject, getForEntity, postForObject, postForEntity, put, delete, patchForObject or exchange whose arguments contain a string literal is a call site (for exchange the verb is read from HttpMethod.X). A call named uri with a string argument is a WebClient call; the verb is the .get(), .post(), ... found in the receiver chain. The first string is resolved by _template (line 77): a leading $name or ${name} is the base and must be a config-bound property; an absolute URL supplies a host; other templates become {}. Example: restTemplate.getForObject("$billingUrl/invoices/$id", Invoice::class.java) with @Value("\${billing.url}") lateinit var billingUrl: String gives CallSite(method="GET", norm_path="/invoices/{}", base_kind="config_key", base_value="billing.url").
  7. Test HTTP calls. In a test file, a call named get, post, put, delete or patch whose first string starts with / is an http_calls entry; this covers both perform(get("/x")) and the Kotlin DSL mockMvc.get("/x").

Edge cases and failure handling

  • A file with no class, object or function returns without setting files_analysed; it is recorded as failed ("Kotlin source does not parse") only if the tree also has errors. Files that do contain declarations are analysed even with syntax errors.
  • Top-level functions (outside any class or object) are not extracted: only functions in a class body are visited (line 129).
  • Call recognition is by method name only; the receiver's type is not checked. A call such as cache.put("users", x) or repo.delete("x") therefore yields a call site with no path and the reason "path does not start with a literal segment".
  • The inter-class text used for class annotations (line 112) includes imports, comments and top-level functions, so an @Service or @RestController mentioned there (for example in a KDoc comment) is treated as an annotation of the next top-level class.
  • Only one wrapper is removed from a return type, so ResponseEntity<List<Owner>> yields no response schema (the remainder fails the simple-name check at line 195).
  • Constructor parameters of nested classes are also visited when processing the outer class, because walk(cls) covers the whole subtree (line 118).
  • Not extracted: OpenFeign clients, Kafka topics, JPA entities, error statuses, properties declared in the class body, and function return types in signatures (returns is always None).
  • The two regexes at lines 104 and 105 match the same declarations; the second adds nothing the first does not already cover.

lang/typescript.py 270 lines

The TypeScript/JavaScript back-end adapter: Express and NestJS routes, class-validator DTOs and interfaces, fetch and axios-style call sites, and it()/test() blocks with supertest calls. Front-end pages in the same files are handled separately by extract_ui.py.

How it works

Parser. tree-sitter with three grammars chosen by extension (_parser, line 29): TSX for .tsx, TypeScript for .ts, JavaScript for everything else. Decorator arguments and option objects are read as text with regular expressions.

  1. Base variables. Every variable whose initialiser is process.env.X or process.env["X"] maps to ("config_key", "X"); one initialised with an absolute URL literal maps to ("host", hostname).
  2. Classes. For each class, decorators are read from the class node and from a wrapping export statement (_dec returns name and argument text). Members are walked in order; decorators that appear as separate members are held in pending and applied to the next member.
    • A field becomes a DTO field (_dto_field, line 90). It is required unless the name is followed by ? or it has @IsOptional.
    • A method becomes a Function named Class.method. If the class has @Controller and the method has @Get, @Post, @Put, @Delete or @Patch, it is a NestJS endpoint: path is the @Controller string joined with the method decorator's string; request schemas are the types of @Body() parameters; @Param parameters are path parameters and @Query parameters are optional query parameters; the return type has Promise<...> removed and an array marks shape list; @UseGuards(X) on class or method is the auth marker.
    • A class with fields and no @Controller is a schema.
  3. Interfaces. Every interface becomes a schema from its property signatures (name?: T is optional).
  4. Functions. In non-test files every function declaration, and every variable initialised with an arrow function or function expression, becomes a Function named by its bare name.
  5. Express routes. A call X.get|post|put|delete|patch("/path", ..., handler) with at least two arguments is an endpoint when the receiver matches ROUTERS (line 26: app, router, server, api, r, routes, or any name ending in Router/router). A handler given as an identifier is referenced by name; an inline handler becomes a synthetic function named like get /orders/:id. Middle arguments whose text matches auth|guard|jwt|passport|token|role are recorded as auth markers.
  6. Tests. In a test file (.test. / .spec. or under __tests__), each it("name", fn) or test("name", fn) becomes a TestCase with id name@line; supertest calls of the form request(app).get("/path") are its http_calls.

The URL is resolved by _template (line 47): a string literal (absolute URL gives a host, a leading / gives a path with no base); a template string whose leading ${...} is the base (process.env.X or a base variable) and whose other substitutions become {}; or a + concatenation whose first operand resolves as a base. Example: with const ORDERS = process.env.ORDERS_URL;, axios.get(`${ORDERS}/orders/${id}`) gives CallSite(method="GET", norm_path="/orders/{}", base_kind="config_key", base_value="ORDERS_URL").

Edge cases and failure handling

  • There is no syntax-error check: files_analysed is set to 1 for every file (line 129) and nothing is ever added to files_failed.
  • scan_calls walks the whole subtree of a function, including nested functions, and nested functions are also scanned on their own (lines 239-249 and 262). A call inside a nested arrow function or inline route handler is therefore emitted as a call site once per enclosing function, with the same id repo:path:line and different function_id values.
  • A + concatenation resolves only when its first operand is a known base. "/orders/" + id starts with a literal, so it falls through to "URL expression not statically resolvable" (lines 73-79), unlike the C# and Go adapters, which accept a leading literal.
  • Function ids use the bare name, so two nested functions with the same name in one file share an id.
  • The optional-field test looks for ? in the member text before its first : (lines 193 and 236). Because that text includes any decorators on the field, a ? or : inside a decorator argument (for example @Matches(/a?b/) or @IsString({ each: true })) can give the wrong answer.
  • Pending member-level decorators are cleared after a method and after other member kinds, but not after a field (lines 188-193); whether this matters depends on whether the grammar places field decorators as separate class-body members, which this code alone does not show.
  • Express routes with a non-literal path, or on a receiver whose name does not match ROUTERS, are not recognised and no unknown is recorded.
  • In test files no functions are extracted from declarations, and outbound calls inside it/test blocks are not recorded as call sites.

Tenancy, identity and persistence

This layer decides whose data a piece of code may touch and where that data is kept. db.py opens PostgreSQL transactions that are bound to one tenant (a tenant is one customer organisation), and the database itself enforces the boundary with row-level security; store.py, tenantconfig.py, identity.py and secretstore.py are the tenant-scoped stores built on those transactions. tenants.py, saas.py, retention.py, backup.py and kms.py are the operator side: lifecycle, plan limits, data residency, retention, backup and customer-managed keys, most of which run with a separate admin connection that the request path never holds. rbac.py is the static table that says which role may call which route.

db.py 109 lines

The only place that opens PostgreSQL connections for the runtime: a connection pool whose transactions are bound to exactly one tenant, the migration runner, and a minimal tenant registry helper. It also defines the two database roles the service runs as.

How it works

The isolation model is "shared schema, tenant column, forced row-level security (RLS)". Every tenant-owned table has a tenant_id uuid column and a policy named tenant_isolation that only lets a statement see or write rows whose tenant_id equals the transaction setting tia.tenant_id. RLS is a PostgreSQL feature that filters rows inside the database, so application code that forgets a where tenant_id = ... still cannot read another tenant's rows. The tables use force row level security, which makes the policy apply to the table owner as well.

Database.tx(tenant_id) (line 38) is the tenant-bound transaction. It runs in this order:

  1. Takes a connection from the psycopg_pool.ConnectionPool (created at line 35 with min_size=1, max_size=10 by default, timeout=30 seconds).
  2. Opens a transaction.
  3. Parses the tenant id with uuid.UUID(str(tenant_id)), so a value that is not a UUID raises ValueError before any SQL runs, then executes select set_config('tia.tenant_id', %s, true). The third argument true makes the setting local to the transaction, so it disappears at commit or rollback and cannot leak to the next user of the pooled connection.
  4. Yields the connection. Everything the caller executes is filtered by the RLS policies.
@contextmanager
def tx(self, tenant_id):
    with self.pool.connection() as conn:
        with conn.transaction():
            conn.execute("select set_config('tia.tenant_id', %s, true)", (str(uuid.UUID(str(tenant_id))),))
            yield conn

Database.plain() (line 46) is the same thing with the setting forced to the empty string. The policies compare against nullif(current_setting('tia.tenant_id', true), '')::uuid, which is NULL for an empty setting, so such a transaction sees no tenant rows at all. It exists only to call the SECURITY DEFINER lookup functions (functions that run with their creator's privileges) which find the tenant a caller belongs to before any tenant is known.

Two roles are defined as constants: APP_ROLE = "tia_app" (the API server) and WORKER_ROLE = "tia_worker" (the job worker). migrate() (line 58) creates them with nosuperuser nobypassrls nocreatedb nocreaterole, or resets the password if they exist, and runs grant tia_app to tia_worker so the worker inherits every privilege of the application role. The worker password defaults to the application password when none is given.

migrate() then applies the SQL files in tia/migrations/:

  1. Connects with the admin DSN (DSN = database connection string) in autocommit mode.
  2. Creates schema_migrations (version text primary key, applied_at timestamptz) if it is missing and reads the versions already applied.
  3. For each *.sql file in sorted file-name order whose stem (file name without .sql) is not recorded, executes the whole file and inserts the stem into schema_migrations inside one transaction, so a failing migration leaves no partial schema.
  4. Returns the list of versions applied in this call.

RUNTIME_TABLES (line 22) lists the 25 tenant-owned tables the runtime role may read. The comment says tests/test_tenants.py compares it against the catalog so that a migration adding a tenant table cannot be forgotten.

The migrations this module applies, and what the Python code relies on from each:

Tenant tables repositories, extractions, analyses and audit_log (seq, ts and payload stored as text so the hash can be recomputed exactly, prev_hash, hash). Edge cases and failure handling

  • A tenant id that is not a UUID fails in Python (ValueError) before the pool connection executes anything.
  • Role names and passwords are composed with psycopg.sql.Identifier and sql.Literal (line 66), not string formatting, because create role cannot take bound parameters.
  • TenantAdmin.ensure uses on conflict (name) do update set name = excluded.name purely so that returning id, status yields the existing row; it then raises ValueError if the status is offboarding or offboarded.
  • Migrations are ordered only by file name; the numeric prefix is what keeps the order stable.
  • Database does nothing special for the admin role. When other modules pass an admin DSN to Database (for example retention.py and the secret store's command line), the same set_config still runs, which matters because forced RLS also applies to a table owner that is not a superuser.

store.py 329 lines

The tenant-scoped persistence object used by the engine and the API: cached extraction results, graph versions and heads, relationships, evidence snapshots, analysis reports, the repository registry, the versioned configuration, and the tamper-evident audit log. One Store instance serves one tenant.

How it works

A Store holds a Database, a tenant id and an optional audit key (line 89). Every method opens self.db.tx(self.tenant_id), so RLS is the guard against cross-tenant access; most queries therefore filter only by key (where key = %s), while the audit queries and find_analyses also name tenant_id explicitly. JSON documents are written through psycopg.types.json.Jsonb.

Extract cache key. Store._key (line 96) builds repo@commit#extractor_version and appends |strip=a,b and |excl=x,y when gateway prefixes or exclude globs were used, so the same commit extracted under a different configuration is a different cache row.

The row cache. _RowCache (line 35) is a process-wide, least-recently-used cache of decoded large rows (repository extracts and graph versions), stored in the module global ROW_CACHE. Its size limit comes from the environment variable TIA_CACHE_MB (default 256; 0 disables it; a non-numeric value falls back to 256 MB). The mechanism in Store._cached_row (line 101):

  1. The cache key is (tenant_id, kind, key), so one tenant can never be served another tenant's entry.
  2. Inside a tenant-bound transaction it reads only xmin::text of the row. xmin is PostgreSQL's hidden column holding the id of the transaction that last wrote the row, so it changes whenever the row is rewritten. If the row is absent the result is None.
  3. ROW_CACHE.get(key, version) returns a hit only when the stored version equals that xmin. Entries are kept as pickle bytes and unpickled on every hit, so each reader gets its own object and may mutate it.
  4. On a miss it selects data, xmin::text in the same transaction and calls ROW_CACHE.put.

put (line 56) refuses any object whose pickled size exceeds a quarter of the limit, replaces an existing entry while adjusting used, and evicts from the oldest end until used <= max. A threading.Lock guards the dictionary; pickling and unpickling happen outside it.

Write semantics per table (the on conflict clause decides what a second write does):

MethodTableOn conflict
put_extract (122)extractionsOverwrites data.
put_file_extracts (154)file_extractsdo nothing, or overwrite when overwrite=True (an explicit rescan).
put_graph (167)graph_versionsdo nothing (versions are immutable), or overwrite stats and data when overwrite=True.
set_head (185)graph_headsLocks the current row with for update; returns False if it already points at the graph; otherwise upserts and moves the old id into previous_id.
put_relationships (206)relationshipsOverwrites state, data, updated_at.
put_snapshot (221)evidence_snapshotsdo nothing.
put_analysis (232)analysesOverwrites data and resets created_at to now().
put_repo (268)repositoriesOverwrites data with repo.public().

The audit hash chain. The audit log is a per-tenant sequence of entries where each entry stores the hash of the one before it, so removing or editing any entry breaks every later hash. The hash function (line 29):

def _chain_hash(prev, seq, ts, event, payload, key=None):
    msg = f"{prev}|{seq}|{ts}|{event}|{payload}".encode("utf-8")
    return hmac.new(key, msg, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest() if key else hashlib.sha256(msg).hexdigest()

With an audit key the hash is an HMAC-SHA256, so someone who can edit database rows but does not hold the key cannot recompute a valid chain. Without a key it is plain SHA-256. Store.audit(event, **fields) (line 293) appends an entry:

  1. Reads the context variable current_principal (from identity.py). If a principal is set, its kind is not system, and the caller did not pass actor, it adds actor = principal.actor.
  2. Runs the fields through scrub_json(fields, generic_everywhere=True), so an audit record never carries a secret.
  3. Serialises the fields canonically: json.dumps(..., sort_keys=True, separators=(",", ":"), default=str). This exact text is stored in payload and hashed.
  4. Takes a UTC timestamp with microsecond precision as an ISO string.
  5. In a tenant transaction, takes pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtextextended(tenant_id, 0)). This transaction-scoped advisory lock serialises appends for the tenant, so two writers cannot both read the same last entry.
  6. Reads the last entry (order by seq desc limit 1). If there is none, it reads audit_anchors: retention may have purged every entry, and the chain must continue from the anchor.
  7. Sets seq = last.seq + 1 and prev = last.hash, or seq = 1 and prev = "GENESIS" when neither exists, and inserts the row with its computed hash.

verify_audit() (line 314) reads all entries in order plus the anchor. It starts from (anchor.hash, anchor.seq + 1) when an anchor exists, otherwise ("GENESIS", 1), and for each entry checks three things: the sequence number is the expected one (no gap), prev_hash equals the previous hash, and the stored hash equals the recomputed one. The first failure returns {"ok": False, "entries": n, "first_bad_seq": seq, "anchor": ...}; success returns ok: True with first_bad_seq: None. The anchor is reported as {"seq", "purged_before"} or None.

Edge cases and failure handling

  • delete_repo also deletes the repository's rows in repo_access, repo_grants, scm_permissions and scm_sync_status in the same transaction. It does not delete cached extracts, graphs or analyses.
  • The runtime role has no update or delete privilege on audit_log and only select on audit_anchors, so the append-only property does not depend on this code.
  • A row-cache entry is never trusted without the xmin comparison, so a full rescan or a rebuilt graph is picked up immediately; the cost of a hit is one small query.
  • audit_events merges the payload keys into the returned dictionary after seq, ts and event, so a payload field with one of those names would replace the column value in the output.
  • The section comment at line 231 calls analysis results immutable, but put_analysis overwrites an existing id; immutability of reports is not enforced here.
  • get_repo and list_repos import Repo inside the function, which avoids a circular import with repos.py.

tenants.py 532 lines

The operator's tenant lifecycle tool: create, suspend, resume, set quota, plan limits, region and customer key, map source-code-host installations, bootstrap identity, and offboard a tenant with a verified deletion and a hashed certificate. It runs with admin credentials as python -m tia.tenants.

How it works

Class Tenants (line 96) opens a fresh psycopg.connect(admin_dsn) for each operation; leaving the with block commits. A tenant can be referenced by id or name: _resolve (line 104) first tries to parse the reference as a UUID and falls back to a name lookup, raising TenantError for an unknown tenant. Every change writes a row to tenant_events through _event (line 114); that table holds metadata only and is kept after offboarding.

Lifecycle states. The status column allows four values. The transitions this module performs:

FromToMethodCondition and effect
(none)activecreate (118)Name must match ^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]{0,62}$; quota may not be negative; the name must not exist in any status. Event tenant_created.
activesuspendedsuspend (148)A non-blank reason is required. Sets suspended_at and suspended_reason. Event tenant_suspended.
suspendedactiveresume (159)Clears the suspension columns. Event tenant_resumed.
any but offboardedoffboardingoffboard (397)Set and committed before anything is deleted. Event offboarding_started.
offboardingoffboardedoffboardOnly after an independent verification finds nothing left. Event offboarded.

Offboarding with verified deletion (offboard, line 397) runs in this order:

  1. Resolves the tenant and compares confirm_name with the tenant's name; a mismatch raises TenantError ("nothing was deleted").
  2. If the tenant is already offboarded, returns the stored certificate unchanged (the operation is idempotent).
  3. If the status is not yet offboarding, sets it and records offboarding_started with the previous status and the resolved workspace paths. Then c.commit() (line 412): from here the tenant is blocked whatever happens next.
  4. Sets tia.tenant_id for the session (set_config(..., false)) so forced RLS lets the admin connection see the rows.
  5. Discovers the tenant's tables with tenant_tables(c) (line 54): every base table in schema public that has a tenant_id column, read from information_schema. Nothing is hard-coded, so a table added by a later migration is included automatically.
  6. Counts rows per table (rows_before), remembers the audit chain head (seq, hash), deletes from every table (rows_deleted), and commits.
  7. Deletes each workspace directory with remove_workspace (line 80).
  8. Calls verify_deletion (line 384), which opens a new connection, re-counts every tenant table and checks that no workspace path exists. If anything remains it raises TenantError; the tenant stays offboarding and the command can be run again.
  9. Builds the certificate: version: 1, tenant id and name, requested_at (time of the last offboarding_started event), completed_at, the per-table counts, the workspace results, audit_chain: {entries, head_hash}, and a note of what is retained (the registry tombstone and tenant_events).
  10. Adds certificate_hash = SHA-256 of json.dumps(cert, sort_keys=True), stores the certificate in tenants.deletion_certificate, sets status offboarded and offboarded_at, clears suspended_reason, and records the offboarded event with the hash and the total rows deleted.

certificate_valid(cert) (line 444) recomputes the hash over every field except certificate_hash. Because the registry row is kept and create refuses an existing name, a tenant name is never reused.

Other operator settings.

  • set_quota (169): stores storage_quota_bytes (None = unlimited); refused for a tenant that is offboarding or offboarded.
  • set_limits (181): validates with saas.validate_plan and stores tenants.plan_limits.
  • set_region (193): the region must be in saas.REGIONS or None. If the tenant has repositories and is already pinned to a different region, it refuses and tells the operator to move the data with a regional export and import instead.
  • set_key (207): switches the tenant to a customer-managed key or back. It builds the old and new key wrappers with kms.wrapper_for, locks the tenant's tenant_keys rows with for update, unwraps each data key with the old wrapper and wraps it with the new one (the authenticated data is secretstore._aad_key(tenant_id, version)), then updates tenants.key_ref, all in one transaction. A SecretError becomes TenantError and nothing is committed.
  • usage (242): per table count(*) and sum(pg_column_size(x.*)) (row bytes without indexes), plus on-disk bytes of the given workspace directories, the quota and used_fraction rounded to four places.
  • meter (232): the billing export for a month, from saas.usage, with the plan and limits.
  • map_installation (260): provider must be one of github, ado, gitlab, bitbucket. For gitlab and bitbucket the id is normalised to the first path segment in lower case (the namespace). The insert is on conflict do nothing and the owner is re-read; if it is another tenant the call fails and names that tenant (this is the operator tool, unlike the API function which says only taken).
  • config_show / config_set (307 / 314): the same versioned store the API uses, with actor admin-cli by default; a config_changed event lists the changed setting paths.
  • idp_set, idp_list, token_create, token_list, token_revoke (340-381): bootstrap identity for a tenant that has no signed-in administrator yet. They need an active tenant and use an IdentityStore over a small Database pool (max_size=2) created lazily on the admin DSN. token_create passes allow_administrator, the break-glass option.

Command line. main (line 450) reads the admin DSN from --admin-dsn or TIA_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL (through credentials.env_secret, so the _FILE variant and references work). Subcommands: create, list, show, events, suspend --reason, resume, quota, usage --workspace, limits-set, region-set, key-set, meter --month --format json|csv, map-installation, unmap-installation, installations, offboard --confirm --workspace, verify-deletion, config-show, config-set --file, idp-set --file, idp-list, token-create, token-list, token-revoke. Exit code 2 means no DSN, 1 means a TenantError, OSError or JSON error, 0 success. Output is indented JSON, or two CSV lines for meter --format csv (the limits key is left out).

Edge cases and failure handling

  • remove_workspace refuses a path with fewer than three components, the home directory, a drive or filesystem root, the current working directory or any parent of it. Read-only files (git objects on Windows) are made writable by the onerror handler _writable and retried.
  • Offboarding deletes tenant_keys along with everything else, which makes any surviving copy of the tenant's encrypted secrets unreadable. It also deletes the tenant's rows in tenant_installations, because that table has a tenant_id column.
  • If offboarding fails after the first commit (a workspace cannot be removed, verification finds rows), the status stays offboarding; re-running repeats the delete and verification. On such a re-run rows_before reflects the second pass, not the original counts.
  • usage, meter, verify_deletion and offboard set tia.tenant_id on the admin connection; set_region, set_key, config_show and config_set query tenant tables without setting it, so they depend on the admin role bypassing RLS (a superuser).
  • In set_key, if the tenant has data keys but no platform key-encryption key is configured and the old reference is empty, old is None and the unwrap at line 223 would raise AttributeError rather than TenantError.
  • unmap_installation raises if no row was deleted, so unmapping another tenant's installation is reported as "not mapped to tenant".
  • suspend and resume check the current status, so suspending a suspended tenant or resuming an active one is an error, not a silent no-op.

tenantconfig.py 409 lines

Defines the per-tenant configuration: its schema, strict validation, canonical form, hash-based identity, differences between versions, and the insert-only versioned storage. A report records the configuration identity it was produced under.

How it works

The configuration is submitted as a JSON object of sections. parse(raw) (line 146) validates it and returns a frozen dataclass TenantConfig (line 56). Validation is strict and complete: every unknown key, wrong type and out-of-range value is collected into one list and raised together as ConfigError (its problems attribute holds the list). Unknown keys get a "did you mean" suggestion from difflib.get_close_matches with cutoff 0.6. Missing settings keep their defaults.

Every setting, its default and its validation:

SettingDefaultValidation
limits.max_depth8Whole number, 1 to 50.

limits.time_budget_seconds60Number (fractions allowed), 1 to 3600.
limits.max_concurrent_jobs4Whole number, 1 to 16.

_int (line 135) rejects booleans explicitly (in Python True is an int) and accepts a float only if it is a whole number, unless allow_float is set.

Identity and versioning. to_dict() (line 95) renders the full settings with defaults filled in and dictionaries sorted; settings_digest() (line 116) is the SHA-256 of that as compact, key-sorted JSON. A TenantConfig carries version (0 means nothing stored, the built-in defaults) and digest. Its id is default for version 0, otherwise tenant-v<version>:<first 8 hex of digest>. ident(rules_version) (line 90) returns the rules version alone for the default configuration and <rules_version>;<id> otherwise, so reports made before any tenant configuration existed keep their identity.

put_version(conn, tenant_id, raw, comment, actor) (line 393) stores a new version:

  1. Parses raw (raises ConfigError), then computes the normalised settings and their digest.
  2. Trims the comment to 500 characters and passes it through scrub_text(..., True); an empty comment becomes None.
  3. Takes pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtextextended(tenant_id, 11)) so two saves cannot both compute the same next version.
  4. Reads the newest stored version. If its hash equals the new digest, returns {"created": False, "changes": []} with that version: saving an identical configuration is a no-op.
  5. Otherwise computes diff against the previous settings (or the defaults), inserts version = last + 1 with the actor cut to 64 characters, and returns {"version", "created": True, "id", "changes"}.

diff(a, b) (line 354) walks two normalised dictionaries recursively and returns [{path, from, to}] with dotted paths; a list value is compared as a whole. latest (line 370) returns the newest stored version, rebuilt by from_stored (which re-parses the stored settings and stamps version and digest), or the module constant DEFAULT. The persistence functions take a connection from the caller, so they work on a tenant-bound connection (Store) and on the admin connection (tenants.py).

Edge cases and failure handling

  • A non-object configuration raises immediately with the single problem "the configuration must be a JSON object".
  • A section set to null is treated as absent; a section that is not an object is reported and skipped.
  • Unknown keys inside retention are reported once by section(); line 312 filters them out before calling retention.validate so they are not reported twice.
  • Canonical forms (auto_attach false equals absent, retention null equals absent) exist so that to_dict() round-trips and the digest of a re-saved configuration does not change.
  • The runtime role can only select and insert on the table, so a version cannot be edited or removed through the API.
  • The module docstring (line 9) lists the audience values as target and all, while the code (line 34) also accepts readers.

identity.py 585 lines

The identity store of one tenant: identity-provider settings (OIDC and SAML), users, browser sessions, pending sign-ins, and service-account tokens. It also defines Principal, the object every request runs as, and the context variable that carries it.

How it works

The Principal. Principal (line 51) is a frozen dataclass: tenant_id, kind (user, service or system), subject, roles (a frozenset), name, email, user_id, token_id, session_id, csrf (set for cookie sessions only), scm_logins (how the person is named on source-code hosts) and repo_scope ("*" or a tuple of repository names, for service tokens). Its actor property is "<kind>:<subject>" cut to 96 characters; this is what the audit log records. The context variable current_principal (line 75) holds the principal of the current request. dev_principal (line 78) builds a system principal with every role for a deployment that has authentication switched off.

How secrets are hashed. Session and token secrets are produced by secrets.token_urlsafe(32) (256 random bits). Only sha(secret), a plain SHA-256 hex digest (line 84), is stored. The module docstring gives the reason: these values have full entropy, so there is nothing for a slow password hash to stretch. Comparisons use hmac.compare_digest.

CredentialWire formatParser
Session cookie<tenant id as 32 hex>.<secret>parse_session_cookie (576): the secret must be 20 to 100 URL-safe characters.
Service tokentia_<tenant hex>_<token id hex>_<secret>parse_service_token (568), same secret rule.
Login state<tenant hex>.<state>Returned by save_login_state.

The tenant id is embedded so the row can be found inside a tenant-bound transaction, with no lookup across tenants.

Identity-provider settings. parse_idp(protocol, cfg) (line 117) validates strictly and reports every problem at once as IdentityConfigError:

  • The protocol must be oidc or saml. Keys must be in OIDC_KEYS or SAML_KEYS (lines 95-98). An unknown key whose name matches secret|password|private|token gets the message that secrets belong in the tenant secret store.
  • OIDC requires issuer and audience. discovery defaults to true unless explicit endpoints are given; without discovery all three of authorization_endpoint, token_endpoint and jwks_uri are required. allowed_algs must be a non-empty list drawn from jwtverify.ALGS. scopes defaults to openid email profile and must contain openid. Claim names must match [A-Za-z0-9_.:/-]{1,100}; defaults are sub, email, name. max_token_age must be an integer from 60 to 86400.
  • SAML requires sso_url, entity_id and certificates (a non-empty list of strings containing BEGIN CERTIFICATE). want_assertions_signed defaults to true and cannot be turned off. clock_skew defaults to 60 and must be 0 to 300.
  • URLs are checked by _url (line 108): scheme https (or http only when netsafe.allow_private() is true), a host, no user name, password or fragment.
  • role_map maps a group name to a role or list of roles, each checked with rbac.valid_roles. default_role must be a valid role and may not be administrator.
  • scm_login_claims (OIDC) or scm_login_attributes (SAML) must map strings to strings.

put_idp (line 297) additionally requires the name to match [A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9 ._-]{0,63} and client_secret_ref, if given, to match idp/<name>/client-secret. The comment explains why: any other secret name would be sent to the provider's token endpoint. A unique-index violation on issuer and audience is reported as "this issuer and audience combination is not available", which does not reveal whether another tenant uses it.

From verified claims to a user. identity_from_claims (line 236) reads the subject (required, cut to 200 characters), email, name, groups (at most 200) and host logins (each must match [A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._@-]{0,99}). claim_at (line 202) accepts a claim name or a dotted path such as realm_access.roles; a literal key containing dots wins. upsert_user (line 343) then:

  1. Takes an advisory lock on tenant id plus subject, so two first logins of the same person do not both insert.
  2. Looks the user up by tenant, subject and identity provider (a null provider is compared as the all-zero UUID, matching the unique index).
  3. If the user is new, reads max_users from tia_tenant_settings(); if the count of enabled users has reached it, raises AuthError("plan_limit", 402). Otherwise inserts the user (just-in-time provisioning).
  4. If the user exists, refreshes email and display name and merges the provider-supplied host logins over the stored ones. Roles assigned by an administrator are never touched here.

effective_roles (line 416) returns the administrator's explicit assignment if roles is not null, otherwise roles_from_groups (line 224): the union of the roles mapped from the user's groups, or default_role when no group maps.

Sessions. Constants: SESSION_TTL = 8 hours, SESSION_IDLE = 30 minutes, TOUCH_EVERY = 60 seconds. create_session (line 422) inserts a row with the secret's hash, the roles and groups computed at login and an expiry, deletes the tenant's sessions that expired or were revoked more than a day ago, and returns the cookie value and the session id. session_principal(secret) (line 433) builds the principal for a request:

  1. Selects the session by tenant and secret hash, joined to its user.
  2. Rejects, in this order: unknown session (no_session, 401), revoked (session_revoked), past expires_at (session_expired), not seen for longer than the idle limit (session_expired), user disabled (user_disabled, 403, with the tenant set so the refusal is audited).
  3. Updates last_seen_at only if it is more than TOUCH_EVERY seconds old, to avoid a write on every request.
  4. Roles are the user's current explicit assignment if there is one, otherwise the roles stored on the session at login; unknown role names are dropped.
  5. Returns a user principal with csrf = csrf_for(secret), which is SHA-256 of "tia-csrf|" + secret (line 564).

Login state (OIDC authorisation-code flow with PKCE). save_login_state (line 474) deletes expired states, refuses with too_many_logins (429) when the tenant already has MAX_LIVE_STATES = 2000 pending, and stores the hash of a random state with the nonce, the PKCE verifier, the return path and the hash of a browser-binding cookie; the state lives LOGIN_STATE_TTL = 600 seconds. take_login_state (line 486) deletes the row as it reads it (delete ... returning), so a replayed callback finds nothing, and returns None unless the state is unexpired and the browser hash matches. saml_assertion_once (line 496) inserts the assertion id with on conflict do nothing and returns whether a row was inserted, which is false for a replay.

Service tokens. create_token (line 504) validates the name, the roles (at least one; administrator only with allow_administrator), repos ("*" or a list of strings) and expires_in_days (1 to 3650, or None; default 90), inserts the hash, and returns the full token once. token_principal (line 546) rejects an unknown id or wrong secret, a revoked token and an expired token, all with the same code invalid_token (the distinguishing detail goes only to the audit log), touches last_used_at at most once per TOUCH_EVERY seconds, and returns a service principal whose subject and name are the token name and whose repo_scope is "*" or a tuple.

Key functions and classes

NameLineWhat it does
AuthError35Carries a stable code, an HTTP status, an audit-only detail and optionally the tenant.
IdentityConfigError44List of configuration problems.
Principal51Who a request runs as; public() is the safe view returned to clients.

Edge cases and failure handling

  • Every query names tenant_id explicitly in addition to RLS. The test file tests/test_secretstore.py notes the reason for the same pattern elsewhere: an admin superuser connection bypasses RLS.
  • delete_idp revokes every live session created through that provider. update_user revokes the user's sessions whenever roles are changed or the user is disabled, so the change takes effect at once.
  • update_user uses the sentinel ... for "roles not supplied", because None is meaningful: it clears the assignment so roles are derived from the identity provider again.
  • safe_return_to returns / unless the value starts with a single /, has no backslash or control character, and is at most 500 characters.
  • get_idp and get_user return None for an id that is not a UUID. delete_idp, revoke_session, revoke_token and update_user pass the id to PostgreSQL unparsed, so a malformed id would surface as a database error rather than a False result.
  • list_sessions returns only sessions that are neither revoked nor past expires_at; idle-expired sessions still appear.
  • Token verification still computes the comparison when the row is missing only in the sense that ok is false; unknown id and wrong secret produce the same error.

rbac.py 126 lines

Static authorisation data: the five roles, which roles hold each permission, and a table that assigns every API route a permission. A route missing from the table is refused to everyone.

How it works

There is no logic beyond set intersection. ROLES (line 8) is viewer, engineer, release_manager, auditor, administrator. A principal may hold several roles and its permissions are the union. PERMISSIONS (line 10) maps a permission name to the set of roles that hold it:

  • classify upper-cases the method, so the lookup is case-insensitive for the method but exact for the template.

secretstore.py 231 lines

Stores each tenant's secrets (for example a source-code host token or an identity provider's client secret) encrypted in the database, using envelope encryption with a per-tenant key. It also supports rotating that key and re-wrapping it after the master key changes.

How it works

Envelope encryption means a secret is encrypted with a data-encryption key (DEK), and the DEK itself is stored only in encrypted ("wrapped") form under a key-encryption key (KEK) that lives outside the database. The hierarchy here has three levels:

  1. The KEK is held by a KeyWrapper (from credentials.py or kms.py): a local master key, Vault Transit, or a customer's cloud key. The store never sees it; it only calls wrap and unwrap.
  2. Each tenant has a versioned 32-byte DEK, generated with os.urandom(32) and stored wrapped in tenant_keys together with the id of the KEK that wrapped it (kek_id).
  3. Each secret is encrypted with AES-256-GCM under the tenant's current DEK and stored in tenant_secrets as a 12-byte random nonce followed by the ciphertext.

AES-GCM accepts additional authenticated data (AAD): bytes that are not encrypted but must be identical at decryption or the integrity check fails. Two AAD strings bind ciphertexts to their place:

def _aad_key(tenant_id, version):     return f"tia:dek:{tenant_id}:{version}".encode()
def _aad_secret(tenant_id, name, version):  return f"tia:secret:{tenant_id}:{name}:{version}".encode()

A wrapped DEK copied to another tenant, or a secret row copied to another tenant or renamed, therefore fails to decrypt.

Writing and reading. put(name, value) (line 86) checks the name against ^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._/-]{0,127}$, refuses an empty value, obtains the current DEK with _current and upserts the row. _current (line 68) reads max(version); if the tenant has no key it generates version 1, inserts it with on conflict do nothing and then re-reads, so that if another process created the key first, both use the winner's key. get(name) (line 100) loads the row, unwraps the DEK version recorded on it and decrypts; any decryption failure becomes SecretError("secret '...' failed its integrity check"). _load_dek (line 54) caches unwrapped DEKs in the instance for DEK_CACHE_SECONDS = 300, which saves a call to the key service on every read.

Rotating the data key. rotate() (line 128), in one transaction:

  1. Takes pg_advisory_xact_lock(hashtextextended(tenant_id, 7)).
  2. Generates a new DEK as version old + 1 and inserts it wrapped.
  3. Selects every secret for update, decrypts each with the DEK version it was written under, and re-encrypts it with a fresh nonce under the new version. If any secret fails its integrity check the whole rotation aborts and nothing changes.
  4. Deletes all older rows from tenant_keys, so a stale copy of an old ciphertext (in a backup, for instance) can no longer be decrypted.
  5. After commit, clears the DEK cache and audits secret_keys_rotated with the new version and the number of secrets.

Re-wrapping after a KEK change. rewrap_keys() (line 154) visits each DEK row whose kek_id differs from the current wrapper's id, unwraps it (the wrapper must still be able to open the old wrapping, which is why LocalKeyWrapper can load previous master keys) and wraps it again under the current KEK. The secrets themselves are untouched.

Audit. The optional audit callable receives secret_stored, secret_deleted, secret_keys_rotated and secret_keys_rewrapped with names and counts, never values.

Command line. python -m tia.secretstore offers generate-master-key (prints a new base64 256-bit key), and put, list, delete, rotate, rewrap for a tenant. put reads the value from standard input (trailing newline characters removed), never from an argument, so it does not appear in shell history or the process list.

Key functions and classes

NameLineWhat it does
NAME_RE / DEK_CACHE_SECONDS30 / 31Secret name rule; DEK cache lifetime (300 s).
_aad_key / _aad_secret34 / 38The bound-in authenticated data.
SecretStore._load_dek54Unwraps one DEK version, with a time-limited cache.
SecretStore._current68Newest DEK; creates version 1 on first use.
put, get, get_text, delete, names86 / 100 / 112 / 116 / 123Secret operations; names lists names only.

for_tenant170Builds a store from the environment's key wrapper, or returns None when no KEK is configured.
main177The command-line interface.

Edge cases and failure handling

  • With no KEK configured the per-tenant secret store is simply unavailable (for_tenant returns None); the command line exits with code 2.
  • A missing DEK version raises SecretError; this is what every read hits after offboarding deleted the keys or after a customer disabled their key.
  • All queries name tenant_id explicitly, so the store is also correct on an admin connection that bypasses RLS.
  • The command line refuses a tenant that is offboarding or offboarded.
  • The command line builds its wrapper with keywrapper_from_env() only. The API (api.py line 212) wraps it in kms.TenantKeyWrapper so a tenant's customer-managed key is used; the command line does not consult tenants.key_ref.
  • The command line reads the DSN with os.environ.get("TIA_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL") (line 184), unlike the other admin tools, which use env_secret and so also accept TIA_ADMIN_DATABASE_URL_FILE and references.
  • The DEK cache is per SecretStore instance and is not cleared by a rotation performed in another process; such an instance keeps a deleted DEK for up to 300 seconds, but rows already re-encrypted name the new version, which it then loads from the database.

kms.py 208 lines

Customer-managed keys ("bring your own key"): key wrappers that wrap a tenant's data key with a key in the customer's own AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault or Vault Transit, plus the selector that picks the right wrapper for a tenant. It uses only the standard library, including its own AWS request signing.

How it works

A tenant's registry entry may carry a key reference (tenants.key_ref). wrapper_for(key_ref, default, vault) (line 195) turns it into a KeyWrapper:

ReferenceWrapperValidation
emptythe platform default passed innone
aws-kms:<ARN>AwsKmsWrapperARN must match ^arn:aws[a-z-]*:kms:([a-z0-9-]+):\d{12}:(key|alias)/[A-Za-z0-9/_-]+$; the region is taken from it.
azure-kv:<URL>AzureKeyVaultWrapperURL must be https://<vault>. followed by vault.azure.net, vault.azure.cn, vault.usgovcloudapi.net or managedhsm.azure.net, then /keys/<name>[/<version>].
vault-transit:<name>credentials.VaultTransitWrapperName must match [A-Za-z0-9_.-]{1,128}.
anything elseSecretError

AWS KMS. AwsKmsWrapper.wrap (line 94) calls the KMS Encrypt action with the DEK as Plaintext and EncryptionContext: {"tia": aad.hex()}. KMS requires the same encryption context at decryption, so the tenant binding carried in the AAD is enforced by AWS. The stored value is aws1: followed by the base64 CiphertextBlob. unwrap checks the prefix and calls Decrypt with the same context. Requests are JSON 1.1 posts with header x-amz-target: TrentService.<action>, signed by sigv4_headers (line 53), an implementation of AWS Signature Version 4: it builds the canonical request (method, path, query, sorted lower-case headers, signed header list, SHA-256 of the body), the string to sign with scope <date>/<region>/kms/aws4_request, derives the signing key by four chained HMAC-SHA256 steps starting from "AWS4" + secret, and adds the authorization header. A session token, if present, is sent as x-amz-security-token and signed.

Azure Key Vault. AzureKeyVaultWrapper (line 106) calls <key url>/wrapkey?api-version=7.4 and /unwrapkey with algorithm RSA-OAEP-256. Key Vault has no AAD parameter, so the binding is done locally: wrap computes HMAC-SHA256(dek, "tia-aad|" + aad) and stores akv1:<hex tag>:<base64 wrapped key>; unwrap recomputes the tag with the unwrapped DEK and the expected AAD and raises "the data key does not belong to this tenant" on mismatch (hmac.compare_digest). Authentication is an OAuth client-credentials request to <login>/<tenant>/oauth2/v2.0/token with scope https://vault.azure.net/.default, using either a client secret or, when no secret is set, a federated token file (AKS workload identity) sent as a client_assertion; the file is re-read on every token request because the platform rotates it. The access token is cached until expires_in - 300 seconds (at least 60).

Per-tenant selection at run time. TenantKeyWrapper (line 164) takes a function that returns the tenant's key reference and the platform default. It resolves the real wrapper on first use and delegates id, wrap and unwrap to it; customer_managed reports whether a reference was set.

Environment variables:

VariableUse
TIA_AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, TIA_AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEYRequired AWS credentials.

TIA_AWS_KMS_ENDPOINTOverrides https://kms.<region>.amazonaws.com/.
TIA_AZURE_TENANT_ID or AZURE_TENANT_ID; TIA_AZURE_CLIENT_ID or AZURE_CLIENT_IDAzure directory and application ids.
TIA_AZURE_CLIENT_SECRETClient secret; if absent, AZURE_FEDERATED_TOKEN_FILE is used.

Key functions and classes

NameLineWhat it does
ARN / AZURE_KEY33 / 34Reference format checks.

Edge cases and failure handling

  • If the customer disables or deletes the key, the key service answers with an HTTP error and _http raises SecretError saying the key may be disabled, deleted or not granted. Every use then fails closed; this is the crypto-shredding property the docstring describes.
  • Error messages carry the HTTP status only, never a response body.
  • A wrapped key with the wrong prefix (aws1:, akv1:) is refused before any network call.
  • Constructing a wrapper without the needed credentials raises SecretError immediately.
  • TenantKeyWrapper resolves its reference once and keeps the wrapper for the life of the instance; a changed key_ref is picked up only by a new instance.
  • The class docstring at line 107 describes the Azure binding as an AES-GCM tag; the code at line 151 uses HMAC-SHA256.
  • For Vault Transit, the AAD is passed as Transit's context parameter (see credentials.py); what Vault does with it depends on how the Transit key is configured, which this code does not check.

saas.py 118 lines

Per-tenant service limits for a hosted deployment: metering what a tenant uses, refusing actions that would exceed its plan, and refusing to serve a tenant whose data is pinned to another region.

How it works

Plan limits, region and key reference are set by the operator in the tenants registry, which the runtime role cannot read. The request path reads them through the SECURITY DEFINER function tia_tenant_settings(), which resolves the tenant from the transaction setting, so a process can only ever learn its own tenant's settings.

Plan limits. PLAN_KEYS (line 21) lists the four limits. validate_plan (line 36) accepts an object whose keys are in that list and whose values are whole numbers of at least 0, or null for unlimited; booleans are rejected.

Limit keycheck(what) argumentMeasured by
max_repositoriesrepositoriesRows in repositories.
max_usersusersRows in tenant_users that are not disabled.
max_analyses_per_monthanalysesanalyses created in the current UTC month.
max_evidence_records_per_monthevidenceevidence_records ingested in the current UTC month.

usage(conn, tenant_id, month) (line 57) runs one count(*) per figure and returns month, repositories, users, users_active_in_month (by last_login), analyses_in_month, jobs_in_month and evidence_records_in_month. Each query filters by tenant_id explicitly, so it gives the right answer on a tenant-bound connection and on the operator's connection. month_bounds (line 49) turns YYYY-MM (or the current month) into a half-open UTC interval.

TenantPlan (line 73) is the per-tenant checker. settings() caches the plan, region and key reference for ttl = 10 seconds. check(what, adding=1) (line 100) returns immediately when the limit is absent or null; otherwise it meters current usage and raises PlanError(402, "plan_limit", ...) if have + adding > limit. Nothing already stored is touched.

Data residency. A deployment serves one region, named by the environment variable TIA_REGION (trimmed and lower-cased; empty means none). REGIONS (line 22) is the allowed list: eu, us, apac, uk, ca, au, in, jp, ch, sa. check_region() (line 93) raises PlanError(421, "wrong_region", ...) in two cases: the tenant is pinned to a region different from this deployment's, or the tenant is pinned and the deployment has no region. An unpinned tenant is served anywhere, so a single-region installation needs no setting.
